## Day 417

*Date: 2023-04-17 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Underwater adventure*

[Image]

Looking at the effect of the floods, it’s clear we were unbelievably lucky compared to others in Brovary — there were emergency rescues in nearby towns. Some people are still without running water or electricity, and damage to roads, homes and businesses will take months to dry up.

Right up the road across the highway, the incredibly accommodating Fisherman’s Market had finished renovating their yard just in time to see the work destroyed, along with angling gear, appliances and much else, including live bait. Their earthen pavilion – and these are *fastidiously* tidy marketeers – is now a football field of mud puddles littered with ruined stalls and waterlogged merchandise.

Later on, I ran to the newly renovated River Port building ([Київський річковий порт та вокзал](https://uk.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%9A%D0%B8%D1%97%D0%B2%D1%81%D1%8C%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%B9_%D1%80%D1%96%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B8%D0%B9_%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%BA%D0%B7%D0%B0%D0%BB)) to rejuvenate in the sweet electric glow of academia (er, to check on American University’s renovation plans).

I passed a convoy heading back in the direction I came from: dozens of emergency response vehicles, some carrying bulldozers, some with rubber boats and emergency crews. Red truck after red truck, lights flashing.

[Image]

On the way back, right behind me on the sidewalk, was one of the ubiquitous, seemingly disposable Kia Sportages – standard issue to the young, inbred white male – that swarm the roads here, its driver honking, cursing, gesticulating for me to get out of the way.

---

Okay so [here’s what I worked on](https://www.ukraine-economic-outlook.com/annual-report-2022) over the weekend, not that you asked: a sort of annual report and forecast designed to allow people to easily understand our macroeconomic mess.

> **Results 2022**
>
> GDP. According to the most reliable estimates, the decline in Ukraine's dollar GDP in 2022 reached 21.3% ($158 vs. $200.8 billion in 2021). At the same time, the private sector of the economy (in particular, sectors whose income this year was supported by government spending on external financing from allied countries) more than halved, from $132 billion to $53 billion (-60%).
>
> According to data published by the Ministry of Economy, the fall in Ukraine's real GDP in 2022 is estimated at 30.4% (+/- 2%). The National Bank assessed the consequences of shelling of energy infrastructure in November-December - a 4% loss in real GDP growth in Q4 2022 and a 1.3% drop year on year.
>
> The industrial production index in 2022 against 2021 fell by 36.9%. Like GDP, at the height of the full-scale invasion of Russia in March, the slump deepened to 53.7%. However, since last summer, all sectors have shown slow but stable recovery growth.
>
> The mining and metallurgical complex suffered the greatest losses (-71%). Agriculture fell by a third (-34%). In both cases, the key role was played by the blockade of seaports, through which up to 70% of agricultural and metallurgical products were exported.

[Image]

I haven’t seen the graphics yet. Run entirely through the browser (no FTP mucky-muck), the report will offer ways to upload, amend and quickly vet detailed information about *how,* actually, the state of Ukraine’s economy is being presented. Eventually, it will be a B2B PHP/MySQL thing, dynamic, based on UEO’s research.

## Day 420 

*Date: 2023-04-20 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Still running *

[Image]

Sick of war-adjacent content milling, so we are running again. Started in March. Very far, usually early in the morning.

> I’m usually out the door at 5 a.m. producing anandamides in Pechersky district. I see more and more people passed out under bridges or sleeping on benches along the river. Yesterday there was a kid sleeping face first in a disfigured position on a pile of sandbags in an underpass near Vydubytske Lake (the one next to the sand pit guarded by loud, vicious dogs)[1](#footnote-1).

It’s the same this year: run, run, run, run.

[Image]

Along the river early in the morning. For years we enjoyed wind sprints up and down the stairs to the Motherland Monument. But that’s no longer an option.

[Image]

Running in the grass up and down the hills at the ranch is fun, but problematic in the spring because of the ticks. Rubbing ashes from burnt fruit trees on your legs is an unappealing solution, unless you are heading to the mighty Ros for a dip to cool down.

[Image]

[Image]

This year’s running shoes are the same as last year’s. I re-treaded Solomon’s Speedcross shoes for cross country. The Hoka Bondis are holding up, but prob will be replaced early in the summer with Mach 5s.

[Image]

[Image]

## Day 422

*Date: 2023-04-21 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Laff riots of boo and petopia*

Unless [The Washington Post](https://www.washingtonpost.com/world/2023/04/20/bakhmut-ukraine-war-leaked-documents/) publishes *in solidum* the classified U.S. intelligence briefs it refers to we might consider disregarding the write-ups altogether.

Why? Because we don’t trust the competency of the journalists writing and editors redacting the stories. We reached this conclusion last month after listening to Isabelle (the newspaper’s Kyiv bureau chief) bullshit about publishing Kupol’s rant[1](#footnote-1). David has been on our radar for years[2](#footnote-2)[3](#footnote-3). Paul and Peter Lavelle (Russia Today, CrossTalk) dropped by after the Orange Revolution and left an awful impression[4](#footnote-4), and the newspaper’s Belarus coverage has been meh, borderline criminal, for decades.

[Video: https://youtu.be/R\_E\_e9rityY]

Which brings me to [The New York Times,](https://www.nytimes.com/search?query=ukraine) which has spent a lot of time and resources reporting about the bloodiest part of the years-long mess. Coverage has been much better than it used to be[5](#footnote-5). The credibility of the newspaper, which for years eight years referred to Russia-occupation forces in Donbas as *pro-Russian separatists*, was almost zero before February 24, 2022. We still joke about Sabrina Tavernise, who made a habit mumbling in Russian with Noah and being whisked away by rebels[6](#footnote-6). Yeech!

---

I restacked [The Ukraine of Why](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/the-ukraine-of-why). *Do no harm* (January 24, 2023) the other day in order to learn how the process works with [Substack Notes](https://substack.com/profile/7561999-pgb). That’s how we bumped into renowned Ukraine expert Nina and her [horror story](https://substack.com/profile/1401421-nina-jankowicz?utm_source=substack-feed-item).

Remember Nina[7](#footnote-7)?

Here’s a passage from that post:

> Renaissance humanists did not call out *statements*, cancel ex diplomats and then write to their fan base instructing them to ignore their bullshit commentary. That would have been tantamount to sequent calculus - seamlessly transitioning assertions into conditionals when challenged, and then back into assertions when their back is turned.

## Day 422ish

*Date: 2023-04-21 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Random thoughts *

Not sure about the Substack’s gallery feature. Will the pictures be enlargable after publishing this post? Dunno. I took the ones below not far away from the mighty Ros river on the border with Cherkasy region. It’s the nesting ground for a wide variety of raptors, wild pigs and, every once in a while, moose. There is also a wide variety of owls about and scary looking reptiles. It’s my backyard during dacha season :-)

Just listened to Timothy chat up Foreign Affairs.

[Video: https://youtu.be/Qfnza5YmRrE]

Timothy mixes up some dates and gets a couple of things wrong in the interview. I stopped listening after this part about half way through:

> The notion that Ukraine is just an object is the notion that Stalin had, that Hitler had in the first World War. The Germans and the Austrians viewed [Ukraine] as a breadbasket. The notion that [Ukraine] was an object for someone to take has a long history. We contributed to that, our politics contributed to that, by just treating Ukraine as object inside American political strife, American political contest. You can see Z struggling with the situation, because Z is all about subjectivity, like becoming a subject, like acting in the world. That’s his gift: changing the world. And his country and he personally are being pushed into yet another situation in which [Ukraine] is just an object and they’re not going to get anything out of it.

Actually, no. Z is all about applause.

The subject/object bullshit popped up during the summer of 2021 when Z’s popularity dropped like a rock (after he and his top state security lieutenants were caught lying publicly, repeatedly — that’s why the President’s Office hired Leshchenko and Arestovych)[1](#footnote-1).

We wrote then:

> ***Sovereignty*** [***сувернность***] — unlike ***subjectiveness —***neither transcends nor resides in a person, but is a *sequence* of gestures and utterances – a repeated script – performed between those with credible parts to play and with command of the occasions in which to play them. That Z doesn’t know the difference is besides the point.

I also disagree with Timothy’s belief that Team Putin invaded Crimea and Donbas in early 2014 in order to suppress Russian speakers in Ukraine.

Not my recollection[2](#footnote-2).

The big news in Kyiv are [new window dressings](https://nashkiev.ua/news/novi-vitrini-tsumu-na-hreschatiku-kviti-vid-sester-feldman) at the central department store c/o [Sisters Feldman](https://www.instagram.com/p/CrSljz1t3za/). It re-opens on May 1.

[Image]

Ukrainian meteorologists, meanwhile, say water levels in the mighty Dnipro have dropped by five centimeters and predict un-flooding of low-lying areas adjacent to the river this week, that is, if it doesn’t rain.

[Image]

---

I read about the bloody mess in Sudan over the weekend and thought about my friend [Ed Barnes](http://www.edbarn.es/), who (between Sarajevo and the second Iraq war?) did a [project](https://www.derekhudson.com/projects/lost-boys-of-south-sudan/#still1805) about the lost boys of South Sudan.

> Ed had heard a report that boys as young as toddlers up to teenagers were fleeing the civil war and travelling on foot southwards to safety in their hundreds. Quite how many wasn't known though we heard that there may be as many as thousands. A story of extraordinary proportions. Teaming up in London we flew on to Nairobi in the hope of learning something more from local news outlets though it wasn't until we reached the northern NGO base of the World Food Programme at Lokichoggio that we realised just what a mission impossible this was going to be.

Ed and I crossed paths in Belgrade (before the bombing) and finally hooked up in Kyiv/Munich in the early 2000s. He returned after the Orange Revolution and chatted up Tymoshenko about gas for tv. His friend [Chien-Chi Chang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chien-Chi_Chang) from Taiwan came to Kyiv after the first invasion in 2014 to make snaps and several more times after the all-out invasion in 2022.

[Image]

Chien-Chi’s album is here: [凝視頓巴斯：張乾琦的戰地攝影紀實](https://www.mirrormedia.mg/projects/ccc_ukraine_2023/index.html?fbclid=IwAR3jpbGKjK5RHNqHZKmD98UiwwZzJNB_ce_i5ZpJsOG6XaY4ccYqFYqgIX0).

> Why close allies of [Z](https://www.washingtonpost.com/politics/2019/12/13/trump-promised-Z-white-house-meeting-more-than-dozen-other-leaders-got-one-instead/?itid=lk_inline_manual_3) have suddenly decided to open up about one of the longest-running dramas from the Trump era — the blitz of meetings, messages and public statements by Russia’s agents of influence in Ukraine — is a mystery. ## Day 429

*Date: 2023-04-28 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The dragons are back *

In the black mountains, a five-years walk away, there is a giant cave. Inside there is a book, a pleading book, written almost to the end. No one touches it, but page after page is added to those already written, added every day, about all the crimes of criminals, all the misfortunes of those suffering in vain. — [Дракон](https://plantuml.com/drakon.pdf) ([Evgeny Schwartz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evgeny_Schwartz))

It turns out that on February 23, 2022 [The Economist](https://www.economist.com/culture/2022/02/23/to-understand-modern-autocrats-read-soviet-childrens-literature) wrote about the play, probably after reading my annotated translation of practicing philosopher Vladimir Pastukhov’s diatribe on [Echo Moscow](https://novayagazeta.eu/articles/2022/09/29/echo-of-moscow-radio-to-re-establish-news)[1](#footnote-1). (That post was *very* popular. The radio station has, er, since changed hands and the YouTube link has been taken down or deleted).

That’s too bad, because I wanted to re-listen to the broadcast because I remember Pastukhov saying that corrupting citizens to collaborate in their own oppression is a problem for Ukraine, as well as for Russia. The arrest of Roman Chervinsky on April 25 is one example[2](#footnote-2). There are many others[3](#footnote-3).

Mikhail Khodorkovsky (Pastukhov’s interlocutor on The Road Map weekly [podcast](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OoSkD7m-DQs&list=PLNJFUkCCIG7yPeZGbdMyE8FqrK0w1QaZ_&ab_channel=%D0%A5%D0%BE%D0%B4%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%B2%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%B9LIVE) hosted by Vitaly Dymarsky) says in the prologue to his book, published in November — titled [To Kill a Dragon: A manual for start-up revolutionaries](https://www.holybooks.com/wp-content/uploads/Mikhail-Khodorkovsky-HOW-DOU-SLAY-A-DRAGON.pdf) — that it was Mark Zakharov’s cult film, “[To Slay a Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/To_Kill_a_Dragon),” based on Schwartz’s play, that inspired the title.

In Zakharov’s film, the archivist, in justifying his conformist views to the knight, says, “The only way to get rid of someone else’s dragon is to create your own.”

We concluded on Day 18 that Russia had lost the war[4](#footnote-4). We don’t know yet when or how the dragon hunt in Moscow will unfold or when/if the wackos in charge of Putin’s Brownshirts will be allowed to commit suicide. Doomed in Russia, the dragon has already shapeshifted to Kyiv and is testing the country’s leaders for the role of the Burgomaster in Schwartz’s play.

Remember the Burgomaster? He is the one who forced people to embrace the lie that it was he, not Lancelot, who killed the dragon. Using spies, prisons and the citizens’ own penchant for corruption, he took the dragon’s place.

The roles of Henry (the Burgomaster’s son) and Charlemagne (archivist) have already been cast, and Balush has generously agreed to play *кот*.

[Image]

Oh, and I highly recommend [Valentina Samar’s investigation](https://zn.ua/ukr/anticorruption/kroti-fsb-v-sbu-choho-brakuje-u-spravi-kulinichasivkovicha.html) of Ukraine’s official investigation of the **almost complete takeover** of the country’s State Security Service by Russian moles after Z became president.

I have decided to cancel my morning run because missiles are hurtling towards Kyiv at a cruising speed of about 600 miles per hour.

So, we’re stuck here drinking coffee, looking at maps and marvelling at war-adjacent content panickers.

March, April, May, June and most of July.

Iryna and Ivan continued performing their duties as Ukraine’s Prosecutor General and head of the State Security Service (SBU), respectively.

Z sacked them, finally, on the same day[1](#footnote-1).

In August, the head of Kirovhrad region’s SBU office, Oleksandr Nakonechny, [blew his brains out.](https://ru.krymr.com/a/news-nachaljnika-sbu-kirovogradskoy-oblasti-ukrainy-nashli-mertvym/31998007.html) The suicide came days after law-enforcement agencies [raided](https://suspilne.media/272130-sbu-obsukue-zavod-u-kropivnickomu-so-vidomo-pro-pricini/) Hydrosila HQs in Kropotnytsky. Iryna has since been relocated to Switzerland as Ukraine’s ambassador there, and Ivan is looking for work, maybe an alibi. Questions abound about the roles of both in Russia’s failed all-out invasion. No one is asking or answering them.

TB2s are expensive, costing up to $5 million.

## Day 438

*Date: 2023-05-07 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Counteroffensive bloat*

[Image]

Our brains are constantly constructing a set of predictions for what's going to happen next. How will the situation in Ukraine change? Whose information should we trust?

It’s flabbergasting to compare stories by war-adjacent Russophiles versus what Ukrainians actually experienced nine years ago as we attempt to minimize the error between bullshit narratives and what, in fact, is taking place now.

Two wordy whatevers appearing the same day come to mind: [Senior Ukrainian officials fear counterattack may not live up to hype](https://www.washingtonpost.com/world/2023/05/06/ukraine-counteroffensive-expectations-hype-russia/) (The Washington Post, May 5, 2023) and [As Putin Bides His Time, Ukraine Faces a Ticking Clock](https://www.nytimes.com/2023/05/06/world/europe/ukraine-counteroffensive-russia.html) (The New York Times, May 5, 2023). Isabelle and Andrew, Ukraine bureau chiefs, respectively, are on the bylines. Six co-authors, two contributors, all non- Ukrainians.

Lets compare nut graphs:

[Image]

[Image]

Perhaps the most galling bit about the WAPO article is that its authors want us to believe that 15% or so of Ukraine’s territory shaded red represents areas “held by Russian-backed separatists since 2014.”

If you were reporting then on the bloody mess from Moscow, maybe, but not in reality, because Russian-backed separatists **never existed.** Rinse, repeat.

The misleading label was adopted by dumbass jornos and regurgitated en masse, including by [ISW](https://www.understandingwar.org/) warbloggers, who at the time adopted Kremlin messaging and narratives deliberately[1](#footnote-1). They are the same jackasses who in 2021 wanted us to believe Putin was operating within the bounds of rationality[2](#footnote-2).

If you take seriously that it’s important to first correct inaccurate reports about the start of the war nine years ago, there’s no reason to believe today’s views about the bloody mess will be less warped.

We are allowed us to offload aspects of the war in memory from these wacko articles. Always best to disregard news from foreign flaks suffering from imposter syndrome and think tanks worried about hand-waving[3](#footnote-3).

Today we mark the one-year anniversary of Phase 3.

On May 9, 2022, we said goodbye to Jim and hello to American diplomats, who on that day went back to work in Kyiv[1](#footnote-1).

[Image]

Months later, Jim also returned to Kyiv after selling his stuff in Connecticut and getting a divorce. He was written up by The New York Times and has since disappeared[2](#footnote-2).

The USA’s Tiger Team tells us Phase 3 will likely continue well into 2024. This is because Uncle Joe and European leaders have not given us the long-range weapons necessary to finish the job earlier (as outlined in writing by Ukraine’s top brass in September 2022)[3](#footnote-3).

Quite a few people have written to ask how the pot plantation is coming along. It’s not much to write home about (though I suppose I am writing home about it), but things are starting to shape up nicely. We planted some things too early, for sure, but others are booming along.

## Day 441

*Date: 2023-05-10 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*New routes *

[Image]

The lotus bushes blossom in June, after lilac season, so we’ll use a new route for the next couple of weeks.

[Image]

It’s less mileage but incorporates two vertical climbs, 175 steps to Batkivschyna-Mat’ and the incline to the top of the botanical gardens from Vydubichi Monastery. For longer runs, we can skip the steps and run directly to Misto Bridge and then back through the gardens.

An organized criminal gang guards the tulips in [Spivoche Pole](https://spivoche.com.ua/), which in the early 2000s I ran through down to the highway. Traversing war monuments on top of the hill has become risky because of Russian rocket attacks.

[Image]

Another option — but I should prob agree with security first — is running around Pecherska Lavra at dawn. There are plenty of paths there for a good 10K route and impressive scenery. If that doesn’t work, we’ll check out the flights of stairs along the outside of the compound’s walls to Dniprosky Descent, which can be used to either circle back or for getting to Park of Eternal Glory (a slightly longer run).

## Day 443 

*Date: 2023-05-12 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Immortality syndrome*

[Image]

Truly, this view has been art directed on a grand scale: budget no object, die-cuts and spot lam, gold foil and tip-ins.

To get from here (right here, where I am right now) to the road leading southeast to sunflower fields, one must traverse a not insignificant stretch of flat-as-a-pancake fields via a road that is well maintained but narrow, at both ends of which one will find hairpin turns and at least one steep incline.

At the other end (that is to say, on the other side of the fields from where I was then, right there) there lies, at the conclusion of a series of two sharp turns, a point where the view from the road just sort of bursts open, and at once one’s eye is met with a gigantic yellow field.

This year turning the corner will become value-added. Multimedia. I have found the exact spot on the road to punch Play so that we turn the corner at exactly 0:56 into [Trop Vite Oublier](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y2MI83z2qBg&t=60s&ab_channel=EdithCrash) by Edith Crash.

---

A cursory review of the "post-holiday" news feed in Russia has led practicing philosopher Vladimir Pastukhov to “fix a new level for blocking the ability to critically perceive the surrounding reality by the philistine masses.”

In Russia, that is.

He [designated](https://t.me/v_pastukhov/630) the new tectonic shift as "immortality syndrome.”

> The point this time is not that people finally believe in the immortality of Putin - that would be nothing. After all, the sacralization of the leader is part of Russia’s historical tradition. But this time, it seems, ordinary Russians believe in their own immortality, and this comes against the backdrop of more news about increasing numbers of casualty reports from the front.

Calls for the use of nuclear weapons against Ukraine is nothing new. Leaders of the propaganda parade in Moscow have been calling for at least partial obliteration of parts of Ukraine since at least Day 14[1](#footnote-1).

Many ordinary Russians still think nuclear weapons can be used safely, with complete impunity and without retribution.

> After all, Ukraine does not have the bomb, but we do, which means that we can and must use it in order to prevent new victims. The paradox is that now, with each new funeral, irritation grows - of course, against Ukrainians, who, for incomprehensible reasons kill Russians, but also against their own leaders, who inexplicably are afraid of erasing Ukraine from the map, killing more ethnic Russians in the process.

Writing for the [Guardian](http://guardian.co.uk) on May 10, political writer Timothy Gorton Ash [tells us](https://www.theguardian.com/commentisfree/2023/may/10/west-opportunity-risk-ukraine-counteroffensive) not to be scared, but prepared.

> Although nobody knows exactly what’s going on inside the Kremlin black box, intelligence-based analysis suggests that Putin has wargamed and rejected the option of using tactical nuclear weapons, as this would bring no clear military advantage and alienate China and India.

[Image]

Opining for [The New York Times](http://nyt.com) a day earlier, historian Timothy Snyder [said](https://www.nytimes.com/2023/05/09/opinion/russia-war-ukraine-nuclear.html) chatter about being atomized is meant to convince us that nuclear powers can’t lose wars.

[Image]

In other news, Kyiv Mayor Vitaliy Klitschko this week took his German friends on a tour of Russian missiles shot down during recent rocket attacks on the capital.

[Video: https://youtu.be/7tnBBHAPJBQ]

He showed off the hypersonic Kinzhal missile blown out of the sky last week by the MIM-104 Patriot surface-to-air missile (SAM) system. More than a year ago, unnamed U.S. defense experts told us the system was too complicated for Ukrainians to operate.

## Day 444

*Date: 2023-05-13 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*The red-haired man*

[Image]

In 1922, punctuating a long, wordy war with Ezra Pound[1](#footnote-1) over James Joyce’s *Ulysses,* (George) Bernard Shaw wrote this[2](#footnote-2):

**Irish talent, when it is serious, belongs to the big world and must behave itself accordingly.**

Shaw died in 1950 after falling off a ladder while trimming a tree on his property at Ayot St. Lawrence in Hertfordshire, outside of London. He was 94. His last words were, “Dying is easy, comedy is hard.”

---

It *is* funny how many people, on learning that I’m Irish, presume I must therefore have an opinion on Shaw. As it happens I do, but the likelihood of any Irishman having one is up there with, I don’t know, a Ukrainian with something to say about Daniil Kharms, because, as accomplished individuals from different civilizations, comparing their politics and sense of humor would be, er, absurd.

Kharms, we recall, was arrested twice for spreading "libelous and defeatist mood" after the Germans blockaded Leningrad.

According to the NKVD report, he said[3](#footnote-3):

> "If they give me a mobilization order, I will punch the commander in the face, let them shoot me, but I will not put on the uniform and will not serve in the soviet forces, I do not wish to be such trash. If they force me to fire a machine gun from rooftops during street-to-street fights with the Germans, I would shoot not at the Germans, but at them, from the very same gun."

To avoid execution, Kharms simulated insanity. The military tribunal ordered him to be kept in the psychiatric ward of the Kresty prison due to the severity of the crime. He died of starvation February 2, 1942 during the siege of Leningrad.

These men of letters came to mind after reading an *interactive* article about erasing Russian culture in Ukraine, along the lines of "the complete removal of Russian culture and history [from Ukraine][4](#footnote-4).

That’s crazy, I thought, until I traced the quote to Cambridge University’s TikTok [opinion page](https://www.tiktok.com/@cambridgeuniversity/video/7203388444003863814) and associate professor of whatever [Rory Finnin](https://roryfinnin.com/about), the Ohio native whom we remember for an unfortunate [hagiography](https://www.newyorker.com/culture/culture-desk/ukraines-biggest-rock-star-doesnt-want-to-go-back-into-politics-yet) of Ukraine’s top pop star appearing in the [The New Yorker](https://www.newyorker.com/culture/culture-desk/ukraines-biggest-rock-star-doesnt-want-to-go-back-into-politics-yet) in 2015.

Keeping this in mind, and testing out Substack’s poetry block feature, we present to you The Poem of the Day, [listening](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aYFTnJF8aPM&t=1985s&ab_channel=Allwaswell) to the late Ryuichi Sakamoto**.**

**“The Red-haired Man” by Daniil Kharms**

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
There was a red-haired man who had no eyes or ears.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Neither did he have any hair, so he was called red-haired theoretically.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
He couldn't speak, since he didn't have a mouth. Neither did he have a nose.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
He didn't even have any arms or legs. He had no stomach and he had no back and he had no spine and he had no innards whatsoever. He had nothing at all!
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Therefore there's no knowing whom we are even talking about.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
In fact it's better that we don't say any more about him.
```

[[Image]Thinking about...

War and politics

War is the continuation of politics by other means, says Clausewitz in On War. To an aggressor, war can seem like a direct way to alter the domestic politics of another country. When Vladimir Putin ordered the invasion of Ukraine seeking "denazification and demilitarization," he meant killing Ukraine's leaders, purging its society, changing its regime…

Read more

3 years ago · 366 likes · 37 comments · Timothy Snyder](https://snyder.substack.com/p/war-and-politics?utm_source=substack&utm_campaign=post_embed&utm_medium=web)

The 19th century Prussian theorist introduced the topic in his famous treaties, *[Vom Kriege](https://books.google.com.ua/books?id=189iDwAAQBAJ&hl=uk&source=gbs_book_other_versions)*, calling this the point where all force must be directed[1](#footnote-1).

Today’s Poem of the Day is “[Gift](https://www.scottishpoetrylibrary.org.uk/poem/gift-0/)” by Czesław Miłosz.

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
A day so happy.
Fog lifted early, I worked in the garden.
Hummingbirds were stopping over honeysuckle flowers.
There was no thing on earth I wanted to possess.
I knew no one worth my envying him.
Whatever evil I had suffered, I forgot.
To think that once I was the same man did not embarrass me.
In my body I felt no pain.
When straightening up, I saw the blue sea and sails.
```

## Day 448 

*Date: 2023-05-17 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Beacons of Russian bullshit*

[Image]

And then there’s Masha Gessen, who together with Steven Korn a decade ago [disemboweled](https://abdymok.blogspot.com/2013/01/sergei-kovalevs-on-rferl-disgrace-nov-4.html) Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty’s bureau in Moscow, wasting millions of U.S taxpayer dollars in the process. Masha has recently, with much fanfare, [de-platformed herself from PEN America’s board](https://www.theatlantic.com/books/archive/2023/05/what-happens-when-the-free-speech-absolutists-flinch/674069/), that is, resigned.

[Image]

[Robert F. Kennedy Jr.](https://twitter.com/RobertKennedyJr/status/1653772735779143681) and [Pope Francis](https://www.vaticannews.va/en/search.html?q=ukraine&in=all&sorting=latest) should probably also be included in the revolting cast of characters in this disgusting phase of fascistic Russia’s genocidal campaign against Ukraine. The former thinks Z could have avoided the war by promising not to join NATO, while the latter has [praised Putin as a man of culture](https://www.lanacion.com.ar/el-mundo/entrevista-de-la-nacion-con-el-papa-francisco-quiero-ir-a-kiev-pero-con-la-condicion-de-ir-tambien-a-nid11032023/).

The truth is Masha, Bobby and the Pope complement each other. And the funniest joke in the world is that the latter two belong to the Catholic Church, attend daily Mass and believe in a supernatural dictator.

Masha is given to self-created reality, casually misleading with the conviction of a truth-teller. Just ask the board members of [Liberty Media Corporation](https://www.libertymedia.com/about/board-of-directors). A decade ago in Moscow, she was more keen on news about polar bears than links between the mafia and the Russia’s defense minister. Hard news took a back seat to fluff, she fired the staff, [resigned](https://bbgwatch.com/masha-gessen-resigns-from-radio-liberty-in-russia/) and returned to the Big Apple.

These days, anyone who puts their name to anything involving Russia unless it expressly and emphatically damns Putin uphill, downhill and all the way to hell should be doomed. If the “morality” of Masha, Bobby and the Pope perpetuates Ruscism one iota, it is no more to be excused for its own sake than “immorality.”

Why? Because we should know better.

.

*Velmart *

[Image]

**UPDATE:** We left off [yesterday](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/day-449) whining about Musk and Magneto as enemy cruise missiles hurtled towards Kyiv. About 30 minutes later, the rockets, mostly Kh-101s, arrived, travelling at around 600 mph. The Air Force shot them all down, except one.

[Image]

About 23 hours later finds us in a time loop. We’ve been stuck in the same day, over and over again, for most of this week. Russia obviously intends to continue shooting at us until they hit something.

The all-clear-for-now message comes at 04:18.

Meanwhile, the dislodgement of dug-in invaders proceeds apace. We hope the young infantryman wearing the webcam in the clip below received medical attention quickly. The one-minute video illustrates the dangers of close combat with Russian zombies on the zero line and importance of tourniquets.

The video also explains why [Yuri](https://www.instagram.com/nucking_futs_yuri/) prefers heavier, slower ammo.

Something still screwy with Google’s GPS in Kyiv. I have *never* been to [Велмарт](https://velmart.ua/about/) (a discount food store on the left bank). We almost always stick to the right (western) bank of the mighty Dnipro, unless aboard Big Fatty visiting marijuana plantations in the burbs.

And, finally, *[this](https://www.nytimes.com/2023/05/18/opinion/ukraine-kyiv-missile-patriot.html)* from David French opining for The New York Times.

It’s complicated.

## Day 452

*Date: 2023-05-21 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Summer, and F-16s*

[Image]

Gosh, summer has arrived. It’s nice right now: the cat and I are swimming backstroke in a big big pool of quiet. Herself is at a seminar about [the color yellow in culture](https://www.facebook.com/naive.art.studio/posts/pfbid02vaHnu4DW4kyxYzwsUGgm9jfpeTrEcuDLJZ3Cayciv47rmBKVVUxMVeNcDWMkmzQGl), and it looks like cupcake is out dancing. The roads are slow with sedans pulling caravans full of blond children to plant potatoes. The city is overrun now and again, this weekend by hundreds of doe-eyed, dreadlocked wastrels here to get baked and jump around to [music](https://www.instagram.com/strichka_festival/). The vines are beginning to ripen, because the sun is finally out. The dogs, they pant.

[Image]

Team USA’s has performed an about-face on F16s for Ukraine. The decision came after allies (Denmark, Holland, Portugal, UK and maybe Norway) agreed to create a fighter-jet coalition. White House trepidations were fueled by the fear Russia would start dropping nuclear bombs, possibly leading to the destruction of the planet.

The cost of providing Ukraine 40+ planes (four 10-plane squadrons) is measly, about $30 billion for the first year. The investment will be paid for from sales of confiscated Russian assets.

What will happen can’t be avoided[1](#footnote-1). Too bad it took 438 days for the deal to come together.

No one expects Ukrainian troops will be able to de-occupy areas of the country under Russia’s control before October, when the fighter jets might start arriving, so it is likely we will stuck in Phase 3 of the bloody mess for the next six months[2](#footnote-2).

[Image]

Google’s GPS is apparently working again.

## Day 453 

*Date: 2023-05-23 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*El Sordo and cheese licking*

[Image]

Never have. By this I mean to say I’ve never actively *disliked* military advisors and foreign war correspondents. Especially Anglo-Saxons. I look at their work and ask with calm curiosity *what do you do* and most of them never have an answer short of moping about and licking cheese and stalking off in a huff to practice monologues.

There are exceptions, however, and we should pay attention to them.

[Image]

That’s the first paragraph from Luke’s [million-word reportage](https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/2023/05/29/two-weeks-at-the-front-in-ukraine) from trenches near Bakhmut. The story reminds me of El Sordo and his men, Metallica and [For Whom the Bell Tolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For_Whom_the_Bell_Tolls)[1](#footnote-1).

[Video: https://youtu.be/qdlQyNe\_9tE]

(What The New Yorker wrote about Hemingway’s novel in 1940 is [here](https://www.newyorker.com/magazine/1940/10/26/ernest-hemingway-crosses-the-bridge)).

But I digress.

For at least nine years and the last 453 days we have spent more time than ever in the proximity of military advisors and foreign war correspondents. What began as a vague dislike quickly ramped up to mean, squinting displeasure at their existence. BBC’s John Simpson (nicknamed, *Cachou,* French for *booger*) was the worst. He was caught licking the cheese again [this year](https://www.thesohoagency.co.uk/news/john-simpson-interviews-president-Z), even after promoting Crimea as a great vacation spot[2](#footnote-2).

There is also Jason Jay Smart, an American cheese licker who signs his op-eds *Thank you*[3](#footnote-3)*.*

[Image]

Cheese lickers hang out with other cheese lickers, like Malcolm Nance. They are oily people, who are constantly bragging about their relationships status. Cheese lickers like to interview one another, not caring about the rest of the world vomiting around them.

[Video: https://youtu.be/RIQWHRz8mdA]

Smart has zero military experience. He [worked](https://leadershipinstitute.org/training/contact.cfm?FacultyID=62972) with U.S. Senator Ted Cruz. This alone should disqualify him for any serious job. What Smart, Nance and ChatGPT *think* about intercepting Russian missiles is, er, irrelevant.

## Day 455

*Date: 2023-05-24 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Burial of the dead *

Kyiv remains the birthplace of inspiration and dubious celebrity, failed ambition, creepy sexuality, war-adjacent content milling and other really rather funny stories, including the liberation of Belgorod region (population: 1.5 million, 27,100 square kilometers, flat as a pancake).

It’s also home to zillions of one of the world’s liveliest flowers — lilacs.

Stefan has provided a run-down of military developments [here](https://www.facebook.com/stefan.korshak/posts/pfbid032ULZakc35ULaESE2D7fq9iiyiRFSNkwKiFKtFMPQKwE9f2Z6sXLnWdBowAiAjwPcl), and Illia launched [War Notes](https://twitter.com/IAPonomarenko/status/1660724107674001432), a weekly newsletter for his 1.2 million buddies on [Elon’s far-right social network](http://twitter.com). Isabel for the ultra-liberal [The Washington Post](http://wapo.st) also attempts to make sense of the mess, which you should not think too hard about or attempt to understand[1](#footnote-1).

[Image]

It all boils down to trolling Belarus and Russia. The gist of this post is no one knows what the fuck is *actually* happening, except maybe for the kids wearing cammies posting uplifting [video clips](https://www.tiktok.com/@ukrainefield/video/7236336055157378331?q=belgorod&t=1684888103956) to TikTok. Looking on the bright side, it might be a dress rehearsal for the party being planned for western and southern areas of Belarus by the [Kastuś Kalinoŭski Regiment](https://kalinouski.org/ru/)**.**

In other news, this year’s lilac bloom is almost over. It lasted about three weeks.

[Image]

Which brings us to our Poem of the Day, [The Waste Lan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Waste_Land)d by T.S. Eliot.

Here is the first part:

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
For Ezra Pound       il miglior fabbro.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
              I. The Burial of the Dead
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
April is the cruellest month, breeding
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Lilacs out of the dead land, mixing
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Memory and desire, stirring
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Dull roots with spring rain.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Winter kept us warm, covering
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Earth in forgetful snow, feeding
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
A little life with dried tubers.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Summer surprised us, coming over the Starnbergersee
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
With a shower of rain; we stopped in the colonnade,
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
And went on in sunlight, into the Hofgarten,
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
And drank coffee, and talked for an hour.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Bin gar keine Russin, stamm’ aus Litauen, echt deutsch.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
And when we were children, staying at the archduke’s,
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
My cousin’s, he took me out on a sled,
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
And I was frightened. He said, Marie,
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
Marie, hold on tight. And down we went.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
In the mountains, there you feel free.
```

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
I read, much of the night, and go south in the winter.
```

## Day 456

*Date: 2023-05-25 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Apocalypse stories*

[Image]

I turned off my mind and dropped by, accompanied by the guy with tattoos on his neck holding what looked to be the Ukrainian version of a [Box turtle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Box_turtle).

The three paintings I liked the best were ““On the Way Home,” “Searching for the Truth,” and “Fireworks.”

“Fireworks” is English for Salyut (Салют), the name of [the iconic Kyiv hotel](https://socialistmodernism.com/hotel-salyut-kiev/) overlooking Park Slava. Anyone who remembers Craine International Trading, Inc. will recall the carpets on the 4th floor of this architectural monument/den of thieves (circa 1991).

[Image]

(We reunited with the then owners of the hotel twenty years ago in a mildly bizarre pow wow with German ear specialists. But that’s another story).

Anyway, Sisters Feldman recently returned from partially bombed-out Zaporizhia, where they did some urban renewal work.

[Image]

[Image]

## Day 457

*Date: 2023-05-26 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Flower power *

[Image]

I’m not sure what the flower blob in the lower right corner is. My guess is Crimea, without Kerch. Maybe it will better resemble the peninsula after watering.

[Image]

Kyiv can sometimes be regarded as a surprisingly green city, considering it’s one of the most car-congested places in the world.

[Image]

Khreschatyk, the capital’s main thoroughfare, is a concrete jungle in the summer, but window dressing has improved drastically this year.

The shady walkways of the city gardens and Mariyinsky Park stretching along the wooded ridge above the Dnipro river east of Hrushevskoho Street from the Friendship Arch to Arsenalna Metro are popular when it gets hot..

[Image]

The biggest Jasmine bloom in Kyiv can be found at Hryshko Botanical Garden, about a mile away, usually during the first weeks of June, maybe earlier, if there’s enough rain.

[Image]

By far the best places for flowers and fresh air are out of town.

## Day 459

*Date: 2023-05-29 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Grasshopper brains*

[Image]

Journalists are finding it enormously difficult to accurately describe citizens of Russia and Belarus who moved to Ukraine to help kill invaders. There are several thousands of them. Some, including Afghanistan war veterans, have been here since, er, 2014, working with/for paramilitary groups like Right Sector and Ukraine’s security services behind the wire in occupied areas. Others arrived last year and joined the International Legion, Russian Volunteer Corps (RDK) and/or/maybe Freedom of Russia Legion[1](#footnote-1).

They come in all ages, shapes and sizes.

[Image]

The above passage from Valerie’s article threw me for a loop, and I associated misleading commentary by Michael below with grasshopper brains.

[Image]

Belgorod oblast isn’t Russian-held territory. It’s part of Russia. And Michael obviously is unaware of what led to the creation of RDK and its current Z-approved mission. So, Val’s story is wrong, and that’s a pity.

I won’t tell it here for obvious reasons other than to note that [Tootsie Club](https://tootsie.kiev.ua/) regulars from Moscow and Minsk don’t regard themselves as *dissidents* or *freedom fighters*. Almost all have zero hand-fighting skills. As for MMA blowhards like Kapustin (aka Rex) , they are, as a rule, irrespective of nationality, as chauvinistic and loud as their Ukrainian consorts.

Which brings me to to Andrei, who is standed in Norway and can’t figure out if he’s a hero or a villain[2](#footnote-2).

[Image]

Andrei has committed murder, a criminal act to which he has confessed. That he holds a Russian passport is, er, besides the point. Yes?

And this just in from [Politico](https://www.politico.eu/article/ukrainian-tennis-player-booed-for-not-shaking-hands-with-belarusian-opponent/)[3](#footnote-3):

[Image]

Since when is not shaking someone’s hand a hate signal? (As for the French fans, fuck them).

---

When it comes to its northern neighbor, Ukraine has little to be proud of. From 2014 to 2019, Aleksandr Lukashenko was [the country’s most popular foreign leader](https://eng.belta.by/society/view/opinion-poll-lukashenko-is-most-popular-foreign-leader-in-ukraine-125736-2019/). That’s because most Ukrainians knew little about him or, worse, were comfortable with the fact that he is a murderous dictator in league with Uncle Vova.

Besides, cheap Belarusian bitumen! But resource allocation puzzles involving non-cooperating agents where everyone uses the same wacko strategy, are guaranteed to fall apart, no matter what they are. Political upheaval — like revolutions — and war just adds more layers of complexity[4](#footnote-4).

Ukraine, like Belarus and Russia, remains a **sham democracy**. It is a primitive post-soviet feudal autocracy. Democracy here is not underpinned by elections or coordinated work of institutions, but by popular uprisings. Absent them, even the illusion of democracy would not exist[5](#footnote-5). Maybe this will change after the war.

The bureaucratic condition of the country is depressingly consistent: a ludicrous system that’s politically unrealistic and dangerously over-regulated. Here’s incomplete list of state agencies *looking after* domestic and foreign investors, not including the State Migration Service and [investment nannies](https://vlex.com/vid/investment-nanny-law-adopted-854321528).

[Image]

There are more than 80.

Human misery in Russia and Belarus is so appalling nowadays that if we allowed ourselves to dwell on our own governance problems we would only exacerbate our own misery. So, let’s wait until after the war.

## Day 462

*Date: 2023-05-31 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Simplicity, quality, consistancy [sic]*

[Image]

I don’t eat [OG burgers](https://ogburger.kyiv.ua/), but try this one.

[Image]

To put it another way, four jornos cooked [this](https://www.ft.com/content/dfa2b8a4-4747-4eaa-8de6-dd238e45000e) up, and the editors of [The Financial Times](http://ft.com) are a bunch of idiots for publishing it. The real *coup de grace* of this whole OG burger affair is that US Senator Lindsey Graham ate it, too.

[According to the FT article](https://www.ft.com/content/dfa2b8a4-4747-4eaa-8de6-dd238e45000e), Ukraine is getting ready to liberate areas occupied by Russian invaders[1](#footnote-1). But we knew that nine months ago.

*Sources include*:

- [John Spencer](https://twitter.com/SpencerGuard?ref_src=twsrc%5Egoogle%7Ctwcamp%5Eserp%7Ctwgr%5Eauthor), a former US army major who chairs urban warfare studies at the Modern War Institute at West Point,
- an unnamed senior Ukrainian official,
- [Mike Martin](https://twitter.com/ThreshedThought?ref_src=twsrc%5Egoogle%7Ctwcamp%5Eserp%7Ctwgr%5Eauthor), @ThreshedThought on Twitter, a former British army officer and author of *How to Fight a War,*
- an unnamed Ukrainian military intelligence official,
- an unnamed senior western official, and
- Oleksiy Danilov, Ukraine’s national security chief.

Two more FT jornos, one in London and another in Brussels, provide additional reporting.

**About the sources**. John sells books, his own, about war. Mike wrote a book about war and is running for public office. I don’t know the three unidentified people sharing their insights, so their views might be confabulated. It’s possible they don’t even exist.

## Day 463

*Date: 2023-06-01 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Putin's gigantic magic bubble*

[Image]

Wake up — 2:00 a.m.

Missile alert — 2:49 a.m.

Missile alert ends — 4:04 a.m.

Morning run — 5:00 a.m.

Breakfast — 7:30 a.m.

---

Practicing philosopher [Vladimir Pastukhov](https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%9F%D0%B0%D1%81%D1%82%D1%83%D1%85%D0%BE%D0%B2,_%D0%92%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%B4%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80_%D0%91%D0%BE%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B8%D1%87) has telegrammed [some thoughts](https://t.me/v_pastukhov/653) about *Putin’s gigantic magic bubble*.

> The configuration of Russian power is simplified and approaches its original "classical" patterns. The Putin personality cult is becoming (in fact, it has already become) the main one and, it seems, the only political institution operating at full strength, gradually pushing all other mechanisms for maintaining public order to the sidelines.
>
> It is not Putin that works, but the myth about him, his perception by the population as the embodiment of “Russianness.” In fact, the attitude towards Putin is purely religious (moreover, in both directions — for some he is Christ, and for others — the Antichrist). In this sense, Russia has long been a theocratic state in which the cult of Putin is the main state religion.
>
> In this state, faith in Putin, and not Orthodoxy at all, is the main spiritual bond. In a sense, this is heresy with obvious signs of idolatry. Putin's main achievement is the privatization and utilization of religious feeling. It is this achievement that makes it Teflon-resistant and invulnerable to any criticism (including all anti-corruption investigations at once). At the same time, war is a dual-purpose technology: it is both a consequence of Putin's cult and his most powerful booster at the same time.
>
> The sacredness of Putin's figure has reached critical levels, comparable to the idolatry of the Stalin era. The country is not ruled by Putin, but by his image. In this sense, the legend of the double has a certain base under it. Few people care about the real Putin, as long as he lives. This is a very good story for the regime, but only as long as the real Putin exists. But as soon as he is gone, it will immediately turn into a very bad story. You cannot create a new false god in a couple of months, and without such a god, Russia, it seems, can no longer exist.
>
> The process of transferring power to the heir promises to be very turbulent. The Putin cult has turned into a giant magic bubble, the collapse of which could lead to the formation of a black hole capable of sucking Putin's entire set elites into itself, creating a power vacuum. The expectations of the "third Rome" will turn into the coming of the "third Troubles" in this case. The unique stability of the current political regime in Russia is explained quite simply — the religious faith of the people in Putin blocks any doubts and prevents revolt.
>
> Power is stable as long as this sweet dream of the mind continues, but we must remember that monsters have already been born and will soon awaken.

## Day 465

*Date: 2023-06-03 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Ethnogenetic wishy-washiness *

[Image]

[Image]

The above *analysis* by [Sam Kiley](https://www.cnn.com/profiles/sam-kiley), CNN Senior International Correspondent, is loopy for a number of reasons. For starters, he hasn’t dropped the “dissident” label. Secondly, most Russians could give a shit about what happens to their compatriots in Belgorod oblast. The same goes for Kursk, Bryansk and maybe Voronezh and Krasnodar (**not Krasnodor**).

While cross-border raids may boost morale for some in Kyiv, they are probably counterproductive because they fuel enmity towards Ukraine among Russians. They might have some military value, but no one is sure how much.

We gave Sam a score of minus 15 points on a scale of 1 to 10 for his first reportage on May 25[1](#footnote-1). This second effort is just as meh.

Alya Shandra at Euromaidan Press talks up Russian Volunteer Corps (RDK) leader Aleksandr Fortuna [here](https://euromaidanpress.com/2023/05/30/the-russian-volunteer-corps-fights-for-ukraine-and-to-dismantle-putins-regime/?fbclid=IwAR1zwV8l07lJuHqh79B1sXKrM9sI9XzIs_qsYxdjFEfQ6tcdU9p1t-lo2l8). The interview exposes what Kirill Rogov [describes](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bPzipHMZv-s&t=2150&ab_channel=%D0%9E%D1%81%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B5%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%80%D0%B5) as the “ideological wishy-washiness” behind the group’s message.

[Image]

Alya footnotes **passionaries**, crediting [Lev Gumilev](https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%93%D1%83%D0%BC%D0%B8%D0%BB%D1%91%D0%B2,_%D0%9B%D0%B5%D0%B2_%D0%9D%D0%B8%D0%BA%D0%BE%D0%BB%D0%B0%D0%B5%D0%B2%D0%B8%D1%87), whose ideas about the ethogenisis of Ukrainians were interesting, but, er, dead wrong.

[Image]

Lev devotes an entire chapter to *passionarity* starting on page 33. I picked up the book when I was carousing in St. Petersburg in 1991. I’ve being reading portions of the manifest every since, usually late at night when I have difficulty falling asleep.

As for RDK, I would refer Sam and Alya to the project’s kick-off presser eight months ago. They should study the video **carefully** before writing more articles about RDK.

[Video: https://youtu.be/RgnzHwKYL04]

*Blood coming out of all places*

[Image]

Split the **big honey-colored blob** (#eab676) in half, north to south. The little red boxes are Russia’s occupation forces. The military utility of the Belgorod Salient is to stretch the front, north to south, thinning out areas occupied by Russian forces, making it easier to blow them to smithereens.

Stefan has all the details. Vitaliy and Taras vlog about it.

[Video: https://youtu.be/p3cupoHmkgk]

This **“very brief meeting”** between Z and Serbian leader Vucic is interesting.

[Video: https://youtu.be/gSwWxsdV7ks]

We can only speculate.

Which brings us to the third stanza of our **Poem of the Day**: **[Asides of the Oboe](https://hellopoetry.com/poem/14554/asides-on-the-oboe/)**, by Wallace Stevens, who ponders jasmine, catastrophe of war and the collective self.

Text within this block will maintain its original spacing when published

```
III
One year, death and war prevented the jasmine scent
And the jasmine islands were bloody martyrdoms.
How was it then with the central man? Did we
Find peace? We found the sum of men. We found,
If we found the central evil, the central good.
We buried the fallen without jasmine crowns.
There was nothing he did not suffer, no; nor we.

It was not as if the jasmine ever returned.
But we and the diamond globe at last were one.
We had always been partly one. It was as we came
To see him, that we were wholly one, as we heard
Him chanting for those buried in their blood,
In the jasmine haunted forests, that we knew
The glass man, without external reference.

Photo c/o abdymok
```

Here’s the [soundtrack](https://soundcloud.com/drandrewford/asides-on-the-oboe-iii?utm_source=www.andrewford.net.au&utm_campaign=wtshare&utm_medium=widget&utm_content=https%253A%252F%252Fsoundcloud.com%252Fdrandrewford%252Fasides-on-the-oboe-iii).

## Day 469

*Tootsie Club sources*

[Image]

# Article 56 - Protection of works and installations containing dangerous forces

1. Works or installations containing dangerous forces, namely dams, dykes and nuclear electrical generating stations, shall not be made the object of attack, even where these objects are military objectives, if such attack may cause the release of dangerous forces and consequent severe losses among the civilian population. Other military objectives located at or in the vicinity of these works or installations shall not be made the object of attack if such attack may cause the release of dangerous forces from the works or installations and consequent severe losses among the civilian population[1](#footnote-1).

## Day 470 

*Date: 2023-06-08 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Panopticon*

We received detailed instructions on WhatsApp from Ed between visits to Kyiv’s main synagogue, [Tootsie Club](https://en.tootsie.kiev.ua/) and umbrellas at Golden Gate. That’s Ed from smokey New York City, from the Yugoslav wars and Day 21[1](#footnote-1). He worked in some of the most dangerous places in the world, including Sudan, Sierra Leone, Afghanistan, Iraq, Iran, Yemen, Northern Ireland, Sarajevo, Kosovo, and Haiti.

One of the mottos [Chien-Chi Chang](https://www.google.com/search?q=chien-chi+chang&oq=chien&aqs=chrome.0.35i39i355i650j46i39i650j69i57j46i10i340i512j0i10i512j69i60l3.1299j0j4&sourceid=chrome&ie=UTF-8) and I have been kicking around during walks around Kyiv this week — “Out with the old, In with the new.” We also contemplate *panopticon* as a theme in the 2019-2020 Hong Kong protests.

[Image]

FYI about [Tootsie Club](https://en.tootsie.kiev.ua/), new Martial Law hours: doors open at 4 p.m. and close at 9 p.m., until the war ends.

We are not supposed to make public any information that Russia’s invaders can use against Ukraine during this dicey so-called counteroffensive phase of all-out war, so I won’t blab about anything remotely confidential.

Stefan provides [military operational awareness](https://substack.com/@stefankorshak) on a regular basis you won’t find here. We concluded early last year that what will happen can’t be avoided[2](#footnote-2).

[Image]

I watched bits of Z’s 90-minute press conference with Ukrainian journalists the other day. Ouch!

This part of the [pow wow](https://bit.ly/3OXBboq) was interesting:

[Image]

It gists to:

“The people found criminally responsible for letting Russian invaders cakewalk into southern Ukraine at the start of the all-out invasion will be punished, according to law. Investigations are underway. I think I know what happened.”

Finally, big news: [Stefaniia](https://www.reuters.com/world/europe/uk-foreign-minister-says-ukraine-dam-blast-result-russian-invasion-2023-06-06/) successfully defended her thesis, in Ukrainian, becoming one more third generation college graduate from our clan of Irish and Belarusian bricklayers.

Congratulations!

We are smack dab in the middle of a catastrophe of such magnitude that there is no long-term development scenario. **Not one.** Anyone who thinks there is plenty of time to think the mess through should have their head examined. We are at stage five of a four-stage disaster management response[1](#footnote-1).

[Image]

That was Day 30, so why [desperateness](https://www.politico.com/news/2023/06/08/congress-biden-administration-long-range-weapons-ukraine-00100938) now, after more than a year of all-out war[2](#footnote-2)? [Image]

[Image]

In an ideal world, Ukraine would not need anything now. But we don’t live in an ideal world. We live in the catastrophe part of Phase 3.

Now they stare straight ahead, motionless, due northeast, not even turning around to pose or stick their tongues out.

## Day 472

*Date: 2023-06-10 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Conserve letters: Zaporizhia*

Noble sinners arouse our sympathy initially, but less so as our journey continues. — [Dante Alighieri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Alighieri)

[Image]

Somewhere in the gaping void that is understanding continued liberation of Russia-occupied areas of the country there lie previously ignored linguistic possibilities of potential benefit to all. Zaporizhia is not one.

As we wrote on Day 10, it is a difficult place name to pronounce, especially if you add the second ***zh***. This makes it notoriously inaccessible to monolingual laypeople who rely on low-frequency off camera stand-up comedy, e.g.[1](#footnote-1)

> Conserve letters: **Zaporizhia**. There’s no good reason for double up “***zh***s.” Don’t repeat “***y***s” in surnames, except Zelensk***yy*** — not Zelensk***iy***, Zelensk***yi***. Maybe just write Ze, or Z. The same goes for “***i***s,” as in *[Iul](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/weightless-self-promotion?s=w)**[iia](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/weightless-self-promotion?s=w)** [[Mendel]](https://abdymok.substack.com/p/weightless-self-promotion?s=w)*. Her name is painful to look at, much less pronounce correctly, or even think about otherwise.

Lag times for photos/videos made by soldiers actually doing the fighting (**which is always best, i.e. most informative**) along the 600-km long front line will get shorter over the next couple of days and enhance our military situational awareness (which we have entrusted temporarily to [Stefan](https://stefankorshak.substack.com/?utm_source=substack&utm_medium=web&utm_campaign=substack_profile) and an army of milbloggers).

[His latest post](https://stefankorshak.substack.com/?utm_source=substack&utm_medium=web&utm_campaign=reader2&utm_source=%2Fsearch%2Fstefan%2520korshak&utm_medium=reader2) is about the Kakhovka dam catastrophe, with a photo of a wet woman holding a traumatized pet. Lots more of these pictures to come.

News coverage on that has been absolutely wall-to-wall, and that was before Z went there (today) so not much need for me to weigh in on who did…

Read more

3 years ago · 10 likes · Stefan Korshak](https://stefankorshak.substack.com/p/june-8-day-470-kakhovka-emotions?utm_source=substack&utm_campaign=post_embed&utm_medium=web)

Yuri below talks for more than one hour about the Zaporizhia line, which will be *in our heads* for the rest of the summer as they close in on Melitopol. He complains about the national TV marathon, which I can’t bring myself to watch because I would get sick. I don’t think Yuri watches it either.

I haven’t eyewitnessed any big catches so far this year. Vaporware kids still roam the embankments touching up their graffiti murals. Ziplines to Trukhanov Island start at UAH 200.

## Day 476

*Date: 2023-06-15 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Truce or mobilization*

[Video: https://youtu.be/iZn9QI5xfiM]

I watched the whole thing. Twice.

Neil for The New York Times filed a meh story about the [2-hour pow wow with military bloggers](http://en.kremlin.ru/events/president/news/71391), but left out the most interesting bits[1](#footnote-1).

Below some excerpts from *a very strange write up* by [The Institute for the Study of War](https://www.understandingwar.org/backgrounder/russian-offensive-campaign-assessment-june-13-2023) (ISW)[2](#footnote-2), which pretends Russia’s leader was making any sense. He did not make sense. This was a rant of someone on the verge of **nervous breakdown**.

- Putin discussed the progress of the Ukrainian counteroffensive and signaled that he believes Russia can outlast Western military support for Ukraine. Putin stated that Russian objectives have not fundamentally changed, reiterating boilerplate rhetoric and false narratives accusing Ukraine and NATO of initiating the war. Putin added that the West can push Ukraine into negotiations with Russia by stopping the supplies of military aid to Ukraine. Putin noted that Ukrainian forces launched a ”massive” counteroffensive on June 4 and noted that Ukrainian forces attacked in southwestern Donetsk and Zaporizhia oblasts, claiming they suffered significant losses. Putin claimed that Ukraine has lost 160 tanks and claimed that 30 percent of Ukraine’s casualties are killed in action, whereas Russian forces have lost only 54 tanks. Putin may be attempting to systematically amplify and misrepresent Ukrainian losses of Western military equipment to portray Ukraine’s counteroffensive as failed and discourage the West from continuing to support Ukraine.
- Putin indicated that he is unwilling to announce a second wave of mobilization or declare martial law, despite maintaining his maximalist objectives in Ukraine. Putin acknowledged that some Russian “public figures” are discussing the urgent need for mobilization but noted that there “is currently no need today” for mobilization. Putin boasted about Russian contract service recruitment efforts using rhetoric consistent with ISW’s previous assessments that Putin is disinterested in announcing another mobilization wave and is instead prioritizing volunteer recruitment. Putin also downplayed milbloggers’ concern over the Kremlin’s decision to not declare full-scale martial law throughout Russia, stating that Russia needs to expand its law enforcement rather than declare martial law. ISW continues to assess that Putin is a risk-averse actor who is hesitant to upset Russian society by ordering another mobilization wave or establishing martial law throughout Russia, indicating that Putin has not yet decided to fully commit to fighting a total war. Putin’s statements likely aim to reassure his constituencies that he does not intend to expand the “special military operation” further.
- Putin aimed to assuage widespread discontent in the Russian information space about limited cross border raids by pro-Ukraine forces into Belgorod Oblast, drone strikes across Russia, and border security in general. Putin stated that Russian forces do not plan to divert forces from other sectors of the frontline in Ukraine to Belgorod and other border oblasts in response to border incursions and drone strikes on Russian territory. Putin stated that Russian leadership is considering creating a buffer zone within Ukraine to prevent Ukrainian forces from reaching Russian territory, but caveated the suggestion by saying that Russian officials will not immediately create a buffer zone and will examine how the situation develops. Russian officials have previously responded to limited tactical activity in Belgorod Oblast and other border oblasts by calling for a Russian offensive to push Ukrainian forces away from the international border with Russia in Kharkiv Oblast**.**Putin’s comments indicate that the Kremlin does not intend to react to cross-border operations in an effort to preserve forces for combat in Ukraine, despite growing discontent within Russia prompted by the raids. Putin also confirmed that Russian conscripts are serving in Belgorod Oblast and that Colonel General Alexander Lapin commanded conscripts to repel the pro-Ukrainian all-Russian limited raids in Belgorod Oblast. Russian forces are likely deploying conscripts to serve in border oblasts due to a lack of reserves and an unwillingness to transfer forces away from the frontline elsewhere in Ukraine. ISW previously assessed that limited raids and border shelling in Belgorod Oblast have become a notable focal point for criticism against the Russian military leadership, and Putin is likely attempting to address critiques that he has ignored the situation there in order to insulate himself from further criticism.
- Putin discussed the importance of formalizing volunteer formations, supporting the Russian MoD’s measures to centralize its control over operations in Ukraine. Putin claimed that all volunteer formations serving in Ukraine must sign military contracts with the Russian MoD to “bring everything in line with common sense, established practice, and the law” and to ensure that individual volunteers can legally receive state social benefits. Putin emphasized that the Russian government cannot provide social guarantees to volunteer structures without signed contracts.Putin’s emphasis on the legality of volunteer formations suggests that Putin may be intending to either assert direct control over or set conditions to ban state assistance to select private military organizations (PMCs) such as the Wagner Group, which are technically illegal under the Russian law.

[Video: https://youtu.be/JXGrh77ViQA]

According to [practicing philosopher Vladimir Pastukhov](https://t.me/v_pastukhov/668), the dilemma for Team Putin is obvious: truce or mobilization (partial or general). Theoretically, both are possible. In practice, both scenarios are not ideal.

> In the event of a truce on any terms, even with the recognition of the current contact line as a temporary border, problems arise with the wacko (obsessed) minority, on which Putin relied when he launched the all-out invasion. But the passive majority, the basic pillar of his regime, won’t be thrilled about sending 300,000 more slobs to die in a ditch. That decision might make the wacko (obsessed) minority happy, but not for long.
>
> […]
>
> The West cannot unsee everything that has happened. There will be no return to the status quo even in the event of a truce, which could turn Russia’s imperialist war into a civil one.

[1](#footnote-anchor-1)

[Putin addresses the war in an unusual talk with Russian journalists](https://www.nytimes.com/2023/06/13/us/putin-ukraine-war-interview.html?auth=login-google1tap&login=google1tap).*In a wide-ranging interview, 18 reporters asked questions for more than two hours in a style reminiscent of the leader’s annual call-in event* ([The New York Times](http://nyt.com), June 13, 2023)

[2](#footnote-anchor-2)

[RUSSIAN OFFENSIVE CAMPAIGN ASSESSMENT](https://www.understandingwar.org/backgrounder/russian-offensive-campaign-assessment-june-13-2023) (ISW, June 23, 2023)

## Day 477

*Date: 2023-06-16 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Missiles over Kyiv*

[Image]

It’s Friday morning Sirens wail. Kalibr cruise missiles hurtle towards the capital, launched by Russian warships in the Black Sea. Telegram channels say Kinzhal aeroballistic missiles are also en route. Anti-aircraft systems intercept the incoming projectiles. Boom. Boom. Boom.

**[*****Two hours later…*****]**

A house somewhere in Kyiv region was flattened and several people injured, including an elderly woman.

"As a result of the enemy's missile attack and the work of air defense in one of the districts of the Kyiv region, three residential buildings were destroyed, 15 were damaged, including two outbuildings. Four people were injured, including three adults and one child," Ukraine’s Emergency Service said, adding later that a bread delivery driver and a child were also injured and that a domesticated animal was killed.

Which brings me to this excerpt from a WAPO article I read the other day.

[Image]

My immediate **fuck you** reaction to these two paragraphs could stem from the fact that many WAPO stories, like the one above, are written for an American audience, or because the newspaper recently ran a bunch of *exclusives* based on crummy months-old U.S. intelligence reports. I could probably think of more reasons to be outraged, but I don’t want to wallow in negativity.

## Day 481 

*Date: 2023-06-19 | Status: Published | Audience: only_paid*

*Ukraine's kangaroo courts*

**Update from June 19, 2023:**

Countries with kangaroo courts are not eligible for automatic NATO membership. The answer to the question, “Why can’t Ukraine join the alliance?” is five syllables long: [Roman Chervinsky](https://censor.net/ua/news/3425649/sud_zalyshyv_chervinskogo_pid_vartoyu).

---

Ukraine’s “allies,” including Team USA, have been flooding the country with cruddy equipment, according to a report published on June 19 by [The New York Times](https://www.nytimes.com/2023/06/19/world/europe/ukraine-weapons-howitzers-contracts.html)[1](#footnote-1).

[Image]

The unidentified “two people” could be regulars at [Tootsie Club](https://en.tootsie.kiev.ua/), the “**best night club in Kyiv**” [we never tire of writing about](https://substack.com/search/Tootsie%20Club?focusedPublicationId=32760). It’s the home of funny arms deals.

In other news, the so-called counteroffensive has so far been much less impressive than General David Petraeus predicted it would be at the start of June. (Yes, the same [Petraeus](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Gen._Petraeus_visits_%27Warriors%27_DVIDS81032.jpg/2560px-Gen._Petraeus_visits_%27Warriors%27_DVIDS81032.jpg) who had a Lara Logan’s picture on his wall in Iraq).

[Image]

By June 14, David had changed his tune (but only for paying subscribers to the [Cipher Brief](https://www.thecipherbrief.com/exclusive-interview-be-ready-for-a-fierce-and-protracted-ukrainian-counteroffensive)). Maybe that’s because Russia on June 6 blew up the [Kakhovka Hyrdroelectric Power Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakhovka_Dam)/Dam, committing an act of ecocide? Stefan gives all the gory details about how Ukrainian forces are attempting to cut the pink blog in two on his [Substack](https://substack.com/@stefankorshak).

Deepstate’s [map](https://deepstatemap.live/en#9/47.5209/36.3016) of the mess shows some progress in the Zaporizhia direction, but not much. Same goes for fighting around Avdiivka and Bakhmut.

[Image]

Maybe Ukrainian forces will bust through the lines of fortifications and lay siege to Berdiansk and Mariupol before the summer ends. Maybe not.

The *[news](https://censor.net/ua/news/3425540/u_shevchenkivskomu_rayisudi_kyyeva_rozpochalosya_sudove_zasidannya_u_spravi_chervinskogo_videofotoreportaj)* I’ve been following most closely all day, though, are court proceedings involving Roman Chervinsky. Remember him[2](#footnote-2)?

*Set of steak knives*

[Image]

Some time in the last couple weeks, while working on a gizzed-up translation of Ukraine’s balance of payments and exchange rate forecast (you know, the macroeconomic analysis I’ve been updating and alluding to in previous posts for [the past, er, five years](https://www.ukraine-economic-outlook.com/researches), about the unfettered [laissez-faire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laissez-faire) mess that is not running like a finely tuned and greased machine, the one I decided to describe to the English-speaking world, whereupon I was seized by a strange and insistent demon who was of the opinion that simply doing one thing and doing it well was not enough, boy, you need to employ a team of seasoned psychologists that would do several things in a kind of backoffice so-so sort of way but it sure would impress the twelve people at [Blackrock](https://www.ft.com/content/3d6041fb-5747-4564-9874-691742aa52a2) to whom such therapy would even make any sense, and whereupon I tapped out lines of inelegant Monetary Base hooey until droplets of blood formed on my forehead and I was hoarse from screaming *well why the fuck not* at a computer screen every time something refused to work – and of course things don’t work during war, things don’t like to work – and on it went until I was sufficiently unstupid to pause and grasp that having something that does one thing well is a good deal better than having many things that are just sort of so-so, and hey there’s all this time in the future to add those things when and if they *do* work, and I began the relatively swift process of dismantling all those flights of financial fantasy until I arrived at the point I am now, which is ready to publish updates about the mess) I re-discovered the [Kyiv School of Economics](https://kse.ua/), which is a unfiscal mess.

I then stumbled on Timofiy Mylovanov and accidentally read this long [threaded tweet](https://threadreaderapp.com/thread/1670936455764475906.html) about **HIMSELF** on the occasion of the [Ukraine Recovery Conference](https://www.urc-international.com/) in London. And became queasy.

[Image]

But I digress. The big news yesterday, [according Reuters](https://www.reuters.com/world/biden-says-threat-putin-using-tactical-nuclear-weapons-is-real-2023-06-20/) *by way of* [Uncle Joe](https://www.reuters.com/world/biden-says-threat-putin-using-tactical-nuclear-weapons-is-real-2023-06-20/), is that Putin might turn us into nuclear dust.

# “The threat is real.”

In fact, this is very old news, which we dispensed with on Day 14[1](#footnote-1). This fear has obviously been percolating in the American president’s head, along with [unease](https://www.washingtonpost.com/climate-environment/2023/05/22/colorado-river-water-conservation-deal-states/) that the Colorado River might dry up.

Oh, and I stumbled upon Tim Mak:

[[Image]The Counteroffensive with Tim Mak

A war correspondent's open notebook, reporting live from Ukraine.](https://counteroffensive.substack.com?utm_source=substack&utm_campaign=publication_embed&utm_medium=web)

I also started reading a 100-something page report about the first year of Russia’s all-out war, titled “**[Russia᾽s War Against Ukraine and the West: The First Year](https://foi.se/en/foi/reports/report-summary.html?reportNo=FOI-R--5479--SE)**[2](#footnote-2).**”**

[Image]

The report leaves out the part about Ukraine’s president appointing Russian spies to run the country’s security services, which allowed the invaders to occupy Kherson region and much of Zaporizhia region in March 2022. It also misrepresents/misunderstands the origins and roles of so-called “separatists” in eastern Ukraine when the war began in 2014. I was also unimpressed by the bits about BAF and Belarus (pp. 47-48).
